### [Page 44]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Remote laptop operation
You can be away; JARVIS still knows what is and is not possible.
31 / 40
Required deployment
A controller reachable privately, a paired laptop worker, an authenticated iPhone client, secure
connectivity, task queue and ability to resume. The laptop must be powered, networked and have
an accessible desktop session for GUI interaction.
Command path
You say on phone: “Jarvis, start my website project in Antigravity on my laptop.” Controller
authenticates request, inspects worker heartbeat and available app, creates task, logs policy
decision, then sends a structured command. Worker opens relevant app, operates GUI and returns
evidence.
If your laptop is elsewhere
When available and permitted, task runs remotely. When locked, offline or asleep, save as queued
and tell you it has NOT run. Wake-on-LAN is sometimes possible on configured hardware and
networks; it is not universal. A cloud browser can do separate cloud-suited research tasks even
while laptop is unavailable.
Network guidance
Use a private authenticated mesh VPN (such as Tailscale) or equivalent device-tunneled link. Avoid
opening direct RDP/VNC/CDP or JARVIS control ports to the public internet. Pair devices explicitly,
expire credentials and support remote revoke.
Remote visibility
On the dashboard show last screenshot/time, connected machine name, worker version,
in-progress app, current action, abort button and action log. Limit screenshots to authorized tasks.
Recovery drill
Disconnect Wi-Fi mid-task; confirm controller marks task interrupted, does not claim success, does
not repeat an irreversible action and properly resumes/requests re-approval after reconnect.

### [Page 45]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Proactive opportunity radar
Let JARVIS find genuinely useful developments by itself.
32 / 40
Watch categories
Jobs/internships, hackathons, competitive coding, scholarships, research assistantships,
open-source programs, campus activities, developer credits, training vouchers, GitHub/community
opportunities, conferences and deadlines. It should also watch projects you choose for relevant
software updates.
Matching inputs
Preferred location (e.g., Pune/Mumbai/remote), education, actual skills evidenced by projects, skill
gaps, deadlines, travel budget, compensation, event price, eligibility and source reliability. Keep
the profile user-editable.
Reliability first
Prefer official company career pages and event/organizer sites; corroborate aggregators; record
source URL, published/checked date and actual opening status. Deduplicate by normalized
company + role + location + URL or organizer + event + date.
Scoring approach
Weighted sum of skill evidence, current eligibility, growth potential, deadline urgency,
distance/remote fit and trust of organizer. Reject expired/ineligible entries before ranking. Show
“why this matched” and “what is missing.”
Proactive notification rules
High-priority, time-sensitive match -> push and optional spoken announcement. Good but
nonurgent -> morning digest. Weak uncertain listing -> research queue or discard. Never tell you
an application was filed unless the site produced a verifiable submission receipt.
Ongoing supervision
Measure useful alert rate, repeated listings, false deadlines and source failures. Record your
feedback (“too far”, “not relevant”) as scoped ranking preferences and test whether relevance
improves over time.

### [Page 46]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Job applications: end-to-end design
From discovery to truthful application with an approval checkpoint.
33 / 40
Workflow
1. Discover active roles through reliable sources. 2. Parse employer requirements. 3. Compare
evidence from verified resume/GitHub/projects. 4. Estimate fit, show gaps and verify eligibility. 5.
Tailor a truthful resume/cover note. 6. Open site and prepare form using GUI. 7. Review required
personal fields and attachments. 8. Ask you for final submit approval by default. 9. Submit once.
10. Capture receipt. 11. Track status and follow-up deadlines.
Integrity constraints
Never invent internships, CGPA, project results, experience or certificates. If a form asks a question
with unknown answer, ask you. Never bypass a CAPTCHA or fabricate a consent checkbox.
Cover letter/resume quality
Cite the actual job description and evidence from your repos. An AI-generated resume is not
validated until you approve claims and attachments. Keep separate approved master versions to
prevent mixing different profiles.
Automatic vs approved
Research, ranking and filling drafts can be autonomous. Final submission is high-impact and is best
gated initially. After sustained reliability, you may opt in to a narrowly defined whitelist for certain
forms, but account terms and legal declarations still govern each action.
Output dashboard
Company, role, date found, source, fit evidence, gaps, prepared documents, application state
(DRAFT, AWAITING_REVIEW, SUBMITTED_VERIFIED, FAILED), confirmation reference and reminder
date.
Example morning report
“Found three open AI internships. Two match your demonstrated Python/FastAPI work. I prepared
two tailored drafts; none was submitted without your authorization.”

### [Page 47]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Hackathon and benefit concierge
Discover, evaluate, register and prepare for worthwhile events.
34 / 40
Detection
Search official hackathon organizers, campus chapters, event sites and credible calendars. Confirm
registration live, team eligibility, age/student rules, venue, fees, organizer authenticity, travel
implications, prize terms and deliverable format.
Ranking
Consider problem statement quality, technical alignment, novelty opportunity, team requirements,
expected effort, deadline, credibility and cost. Do not over-weight large advertised prize pools if
odds or conditions are unknown.
Registration
JARVIS can draft team information and populate a form through the browser; require final consent
for binding declarations and submissions. Keep a record of documents sent and receipt links. Alert
on round changes and deadlines.
Hackathon preparation agent
Given an event: extract problem statements; compare project ideas; research prior solutions; write
problem framing, baseline and stretch architecture; set up repo/worktree; build evaluation plan;
produce pitch slides/report/video if requested; rehearse 5-minute pitch; verify rules on use of
AI-generated material.
Parallel contest work
Researcher finds data and sources, Engineer prototypes, Reviewer checks reproducibility, Designer
creates presentation assets, Reporter prepares progress. Do not change the same branch
concurrently; isolate worktrees and merge after tests.
Offers and credits
Watch free student tools, compute credits, educational programs, scholarships and public
competitions. Avoid deals requiring unsafe personal information or deposit. Notify the expiry
window and official redemption page; never claim credits activated without a receipt.
Post-event learning
Record outcome, judge feedback, technology used, pain points and opportunities for improvement.
Feed correction memory, not just a victory/loss label.

### [Page 48]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Complete project-building workflow
From a spoken idea to a tested website or AI system.
35 / 40
Trigger
“Jarvis, here is my project. Research competitors, make it unique, create a site using my coding
agent, keep backend behavior intact and deploy a preview.”
Planning
Create a requirements document, read only the permitted repository, identify existing backend/API
contracts, inspect current UI, find reference sites, create a task dependency graph and define
acceptance tests. Obtain your chosen aesthetic constraints from memory with provenance.
Parallel work
Researcher: credible background and source links. Designer: wireframes, typography, layout
system and screenshots. Engineer: implementation in dedicated branch/worktree via Antigravity or
other coding tools. QA: endpoint checks, broken links, visual comparison, responsive and
accessibility tests. Reporter: change log, demo script and PDF presentation.
Protect existing logic
Diff-based guards reject unauthorized edits to the backend or tests when scope says “UI only.” Run
current tests as baseline, change allowed files, rerun suites and show exact differences. Do not
claim all tests passed without saving command output.
Handing between models
Use explicit artifacts and a single canonical spec. A model's unverified advice is candidate input;
another model's agreement is not proof. Ground design decisions in project state and test them
empirically.
Deployment and approval
Use a staging environment and a preview link. Production deployment, cloud spending, domain
changes and public publication should request explicit consent. Store rollback release and
environment configuration separately.
Deliverables
Repository commit or patch, preview screenshots, running instructions, tests with results, known
issues, artifact bundle and summarized next steps.

### [Page 49]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Messages, email and calendar
High usefulness, high stakes: act only with clear send approvals.
36 / 40
Email assistant
Read an authorized inbox scope, classify priority, summarize relevant threads, draft replies, add
attachments from permitted workspace and show a frozen preview. Any personal or professional
email stays in AWAITING_APPROVAL until you authorize exact recipients, content and attachments.
Social/messaging agent
Draft DMs, follow-ups, recruiter replies or posts. Support manual review of recipients and platform
controls. The send action is separate from drafting and uses the same hash-bound approval gate.
Some platforms prohibit bot access; stop when unsupported.
Calendar and assignments
With authorized access, identify due dates from actual messages or calendars, reconcile conflicting
dates, create reminders and provide daily briefing: “Your assignment due tomorrow is still not
marked complete.” Do not invent deadlines from generic text.
Personal communication rules
Avoid unsolicited bulk messaging, impersonation and automatic replies to sensitive conversations.
Suppress duplicate reminders and never forward private chat content to other AI sites unless you
approve the material.
Task examples
“Prepare a reply to my placement coordinator” -> draft only. “Send this exact approved draft to
the selected recipient” -> verify approval and recipient, transmit and capture sent receipt. “Move
my reminder to 8 PM” -> update calendar item after matching correct event.
Audit evidence
Store draft_hash, recipient, attachments, approval_time, sending_app, send_result, message_id
when available. Failed attempts should not auto-retry if they may have succeeded server-side.

### [Page 50]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Live web research and artifact creation
Give JARVIS broad internet reach, citations and document skills.
37 / 40
Web access
JARVIS can search public engines, navigate sites, evaluate information, compare contradictory
claims, gather source links and retrieve files. It cannot literally contain or see “all of Google”;
paywalls, private accounts, robots controls, regions, and inaccessible sites remain limits.
Research quality rubric
For each substantive claim record page title, publisher, URL, checked date, claim excerpt or note,
reliability rating and whether the page directly supports the claim. Cross-check time-sensitive news
and offers against the current authoritative source.
PDF/doc/slide workflow
User intent -> outline -> structured sections -> data/assets -> document generator -> render
pages -> visual QA -> corrected export -> final link or controlled local save. Use source files for
editable documents; don't use an image of text when an accessible PDF is required.
File classes
PDF: ReportLab/other PDF authoring tools; DOCX: python-docx; PPTX: python-pptx; spreadsheet:
openpyxl; code: local editor/IDE; visual assets: licensed images or user-approved generators. All
outputs need deterministic paths and versioned filenames.
Review gates
Check page count, missing fonts, broken links, visible overlaps, citation presence, tables and
accessibility. For code: install dependencies, run test suite, save exit code. For a website: inspect
browser screenshots on mobile/desktop sizes.
Privacy while researching
Never put entire private profile, conversations or credentials into random search boxes. Use least
necessary excerpts and require consent for sensitive cross-app disclosures.
Skill extension
Jarvis should learn reusable “write a clean assignment PDF”, “build a fast portfolio”, “research an
opportunity” templates with version and regression tests.

### [Page 51]
JARVIS  /  BUILD BLUEPRINT
23 OCT 2026
Evaluation, red-team and acceptance tests
A powerful agent is only as good as its failure behavior.
38 / 40
Functional metrics
Task completion with objective receipt; time saved against manual baseline; user interruption rate;
factual/source error rate; regression escape rate; mean time to recover; latency; on-device
CPU/RAM; storage growth; notification precision.
Safety metrics
Number of unapproved sends (target ZERO); credential leaks (ZERO); actions outside allowlists
(ZERO); silent fabricated success (ZERO); unknown-state irreversible retries (ZERO). Measure these
in controlled tests with fake accounts and throwaway files.
Core test scenarios
1. Browser layout changes. 2. A popup steals focus. 3. Laptop disconnects mid-click. 4. User
interrupts and takes mouse. 5. Two agents target same document. 6. Email send requires
approval. 7. Website contains prompt injection. 8. AI app usage cap appears. 9. Deleted memory is
queried. 10. Correction overrides old preference. 11. Speaker impostor attempts approval. 12.
Storage budget reaches warning threshold.
Regression suite
Use known benchmark tasks with expected screenshots/text artifacts and evaluation rubrics. Test
both successful runs and deliberate failures. A new skill cannot deploy if it worsens security scores,
modifies prohibited paths or destroys an approval boundary.
Release ladder
Lab -> dedicated low-privilege user -> personal noncritical tasks -> connected real inbox in
draft-only mode -> selected production workflows. At each stage require measured outcomes,
backup/restore drill and rollback.
What to log
Task ID, reason, policy version, selected tool/device, action hashes, screenshot references,
timestamps, expected vs observed outcome, consent record and recovery route. Redact
unnecessary private content from diagnostics.

90-day build and upgrade program
Ship useful capabilities before pursuing science-fiction breadth.
39 / 40
Days 1-7: minimum viable JARVIS
Create repository, security policy, private controller, task/approval database, browser worker,
single desktop worker and dashboard. Make “research a topic, draft a summary and type into a
blank editor” reliable. Use OpenClaw only after restricting tools and reviewing install.
Days 8-21: personal productivity
Add scheduler, deduplicated job/hackathon search, reminders, email draft-only workflow, project
file organization and stable notifications. Track 20 real tasks; fix top errors.
Days 22-40: memory and voice
Add raw transcript archive, explicit facts, memory retrieval, correction pipeline, storage dashboard,
laptop wake detection and local transcription/TTS. Test remembered preferences and deletion.
Days 41-60: remote and multi-agent
Pair iPhone client, add private remote queue, health monitoring and device status. Add two to
three parallel subagents with file/worktree isolation. Implement forced approval for sends and
submissions.
Days 61-90: integrated deep work
Connect coding workflow to Antigravity/other AI tools through tested GUI routes; add website
delivery, screenshots, testing, report/PDF generation and confidence scoring. Red-team and
benchmark before increasing autonomy.
Rough resource plan
Software may be free/open-source, but inference, hosting, storage, device power and subscription
plans can cost money. Evaluate local model quality/hardware and existing subscriptions before
adding cloud spend; no credible fixed monthly cost can be quoted without workload/device details.
GUI use still consumes account entitlements.
Upgrade backlog
Room voice node, smarter speaker verification, knowledge graph, multimodal camera perception,
local model routing, encrypted cross-device sync, workflow skill marketplace under review,
self-diagnostics, calendar-aware timing, offline mode, adaptive routine recommendations and
reversible autopilot.

Page 54
Part B - Career-Ops integration into JARVIS
Two source reports are reproduced here with formatting cleaned (diagram debris removed, code blocks restored).
Where they say the new section would be appended as 'pages 52-57', that is superseded by this master edition. The
repository path career-ops-hq/career-ops is quoted from the supplied reports; I did not verify it in this session, so

B1. Integration brief (report appendix)
Executive Summary
This report analyzes the open-source Career-Ops job-search agent (GitHub: career-ops-hq/career-ops) and presents
a plan to integrate its capabilities into the JARVIS Personal AI system. Career-Ops offers a rich set of
features—job-board scanning with CV fit scoring, ATS-optimized resume and cover-letter generation, contact
discovery, email drafting, interview prep, and application tracking—all running locally under an MIT license. We map
each Career-Ops module to corresponding JARVIS functionality, identify minimal integration points, and address
data, security, and scaling concerns (especially under 10,000 hostile users).
The plan calls for adding a Career-Ops service layer to JARVIS that invokes the Career-Ops CLI (via subprocess calls
or integrated agent skills), with adapters to translate between JARVIS’s data formats and Career-Ops’s
Markdown-based inputs. Architecture changes are limited: use a worker pool or queue to manage heavy tasks (LLM
calls, browser automation), add new UI commands (e.g. /job-scan, /generate-resume), and extend JARVIS’s database
with application-tracking fields. We ensure privacy by keeping all operations local (Career-Ops already does so) and
by sandboxing any web scraping.
Below, Table 1 maps key repository paths to JARVIS features, estimating effort and risks. We then outline the precise
code/architecture changes, potential failure modes under heavy load, and a testing/rollout checklist. Finally, we
describe how this content will be appended to the existing 51-page report and provide steps to generate the final
merged PDF (final_merge.pdf).

All career-ops code is MIT-licensed, which aligns with JARVIS’s permissive usage. There is no conflict with
open-source policies. Career-Ops is explicitly local-first (no central server) and AI-agnostic, running on the user’s
machine with the chosen AI engine, preserving user privacy.
Integration Approach and Code Changes
We will treat Career-Ops as an optional subsystem in JARVIS, invoked through JARVIS’s agent interface or CLI.
Concretely:
Triggering Career-Ops: JARVIS will issue subprocess calls to the Career-Ops CLI (e.g. career-ops scan --job <URL>).
The snippet below shows how JARVIS (in Python) might wrap a scan:
import subprocess, json
def run_job_scan(job_url, profile_md):
Call Career-Ops CLI to scan the job against user's CV
result = subprocess.run([
"career-ops", "scan",
"--job", job_url,
"--profile", profile_md,
"--output-format", "json"
], capture_output=True, text=True)
if result.returncode != 0:
raise RuntimeError("Career-Ops scan failed")
data = json.loads(result.stdout)
return data
Example usage within JARVIS
job_result = run_job_scan("https://greenhouse.io/jobs/123", "user_cv.md")
if job_result["global_score"] >= 4.0:
subprocess.run([
"career-ops", "generate-pdf", "--job", "https://greenhouse.io/jobs/123",
"--profile", "user_cv.md"
])
Data Adapters: JARVIS maintains user profile (CV, experience). We must format this for Career-Ops (Markdown in
data/cv.md and config/profile.yml). Conversely, results (JSON scores, PDF files) from Career-Ops must feed back into
JARVIS’s data store (e.g. adding a new application entry or knowledge memory).

---

### [Page 56]
JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 56
UI/Command Integration: Expose new commands in JARVIS’s interface, such as /job-scan <URL>, /generate-resume
<job>, /find-contact <company>. These commands will call the underlying functions above. Responses (scores,
texts) will be displayed in JARVIS chat or dashboard.
Architecture: Add a Career-Ops worker pool. Because tasks use heavy LLM and browser automation (Playwright for
PDFs), we should queue jobs and limit parallelism. For example, use a job queue with a pool of Node processes or
Docker containers (since Career-Ops can run in Docker). This prevents system overload with 10k concurrent
requests.
User commands -> JARVIS UI -> Career-Ops service (queue + worker pool)
-> career-ops CLI -> AI engine + Playwright
-> local data store (CV / cover PDFs, pipeline, scores) -> back into JARVIS memory
Failure Modes under 10k hostile users: The main risks are resource exhaustion and denial-of-service:
High CPU/Memory from many simultaneous LLM calls or Chromium processes. Mitigation: Cap the number of
simultaneous scans/PDF generations (e.g. max 5 at once), use rate limiting, and auto-reject or queue excess
requests.
File/Database Contention: Career-Ops writes to data/applications.md and data/pdf-index.tsv. Under concurrency,
lock contention can occur. Mitigation: Use separate workspace per user or transaction locking, or migrate storage to
a lightweight database (e.g. SQLite with row locks).
Untrusted Inputs: A malicious user might supply harmful URLs (SSRF) or manipulate CLI args. Mitigation: Pre-validate
URLs against a whitelist, strip any dangerous flags, and run browsers in a sandbox (Career-Ops uses Playwright
sandbox by default).
Denial-of-Service: Many rapid scan commands could overwhelm local resources. Mitigation: Introduce request
throttling and captcha (if a web UI).
Overall, these changes do not fundamentally alter JARVIS’s architecture—we are adding a contained plugin-like
service. All new data stays client-side by design (Career-Ops is local-only).
Testing & Rollout Plan
Unit Tests: Write tests to mock Career-Ops CLI outputs. For example, simulate a “scan” result and verify JARVIS
correctly ingests scores. Test edge cases (invalid job URL, missing CV file).
Integration Tests: End-to-end testing: feed real job descriptions into JARVIS commands and ensure application
entries, PDFs, and dashboard entries are created.
Security Tests: Attempt SSRF/XSS by feeding malicious URLs or job descriptions. Verify that Career-Ops mode’s
sanitization (and our own validation) blocks these.
Load Testing: Use tools (e.g. Locust) to simulate 1000+ concurrent user jobs. Monitor CPU, memory, and ensure
tasks queue/delay as expected.
Privacy Review: Confirm that no data is sent out; career-ops’s privacy is strict (no telemetry). Check logs for any
inadvertent personal data.
Rollback Plan: Feature-flag the Career-Ops integration; if issues arise, disable plugin and fall back to basic job
advice.
Documentation & Training: Update the merged PDF and user guide with new Career-Ops commands and usage
examples. Provide a quickstart (“Run career-ops init within JARVIS’s workspace”).
Phased Deployment:
Dev: Integrate and test in a development branch of JARVIS; internal review.
Canary: Roll out to a subset of beta users with monitoring enabled.
Beta: Wider release; collect logs on usage patterns.
GA: Full production.
Changed Pages: In the final PDF, this section will be appended after page 51. We expect ~5–6 new pages
(appending pages 52–57).
PDF Assembly
Generate this report section as PDF (e.g. via Pandoc or LaTeX).
Merge with existing merge.pdf (51 pages) to create final_merge.pdf. Tools like pdfjam or PyPDF can concatenate
them.
Review citations: Ensure all `` links point to career-ops sources.
Publish: final_merge.pdf will contain pages 1–51 (original) plus 52–57 (Career-Ops integration).

### [Page 58]
JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 58
B2. Deep research and plan
Executive Summary: We analyze the open-source career-ops job-search agent (MIT license) and plan its integration
into JARVIS. Career-ops is a local-first AI assistant that scans job boards, scores listings, generates ATS-optimized
resumes and cover letters, finds contacts, drafts emails, provides interview prep, and tracks applications. All data
stays on the user’s machine (no cloud or telemetry). We map each career-ops module to JARVIS capabilities (Table
below), describe the minimal architecture changes needed (invoking career-ops via CLI, adding a worker queue, new
UI commands, extended data store), and outline security and scalability measures for 10k users. We also sketch a
phased implementation and testing plan. All new modules run locally in JARVIS (like a plugin), preserving privacy
and relying on the user’s chosen AI engine. This report (with sources) supplements the original JARVIS blueprint.
Career-Ops Overview
Local & Open Source. Career-ops runs entirely on the user’s machine inside an AI coding CLI (e.g. Claude Code,
Codex). It is MIT-licensed and contains no telemetry or cloud services (data stays local). This aligns with JARVIS’s
privacy goals. Any career-ops code or UI runs in the client, so we incur no new data sharing.
Key Features. Career-ops covers end-to-end job searching. It scans job boards or pasted listings and scores each
against the user’s CV on a 5-point rubric. It generates tailored resumes (ATS-friendly PDFs) per job and
research-backed cover letters. It includes “deep” company research (summary of culture/strategy), contact
discovery (find recruiters/managers and draft LinkedIn outreach), email draft generation, and analysis of past
applications (patterns, success rates). A terminal-based pipeline dashboard (Go TUI) lets users browse and filter
their applications. There are also interview prep scripts, red-flag checks, and negotiation aids (salary-gap analysis,
contract review). All features are invoked via CLI subcommands (e.g. scan, generate-pdf, cover, deep, contacto,
etc.) and run locally using Playwright for PDFs and an LLM for text.
Architecture & Data. Career-ops uses a simple file-based workspace (YAML/Markdown files for profile, pipeline, CV,
plus generated PDFs) and a small Go service for the TUI. By default it writes outputs under a workspace folder
(tracked by pipeline.md, applications.md, etc.). Under heavy use, its file-based storage can contend on concurrent
writes – SQLite or a lightweight DB might be needed for scaling (SQLite only allows one writer at a time).
License. Career-ops is MIT-licensed. JARVIS is compatible with MIT, so we can freely incorporate or call it without
legal issues. No proprietary or paid modules are required.
Mapping Modules to JARVIS
Below, each career-ops module (repository path or mode) is mapped to the corresponding JARVIS functionality.
Integration effort refers to how much JARVIS code/UI needs changes.
Job Scanning (scan.mjs). Searches configured job portals or uses an ATS API to fetch listings, then uses an LLM rubric
to score each 1–5 against the user’s CV. Integration: We would add a JARVIS command (e.g. /job-scan <URL>) that
calls the career-ops CLI (via a subprocess) with user-provided URL or text. JARVIS must supply the user’s CV/profile
in Markdown (as career-ops expects) and capture the JSON output. The scoring rubric (match, culture fit, comp)
comes from career-ops’s logic. Data: Input is job URL or description and user CV. Output (scores, fit reasons) is JSON.
Risks: Remote scanning risks SSRF if job boards or links are malicious. We must whitelist known job site domains or
sanitize URLs (career-ops has guards, but we double-check). The CLI pattern is:
result = subprocess.run([
"career-ops", "scan",
"--job", job_url,
"--profile", "path/to/profile.yml",
"--output-format", "json"
], capture_output=True, text=True)
We parse result.stdout for the scores.
Effort: Medium (JARVIS needs to wrap the CLI, adapt data formats). (Table 1, below.)
Resume PDF (generate-pdf.mjs). Uses Playwright+HTML to create an ATS-optimized PDF resume tailored to the job.
Integration: JARVIS invokes career-ops generate-pdf --job <URL> --profile <file>, producing a PDF file. We then
attach or store the PDF in the user’s data (e.g. in JARVIS’s document store). Data: Uses the same profile/CV data.
Output is a PDF in a workspace folder. Ensure to capture or reference that PDF from JARVIS. Risks: The operation is
CPU-intensive (launching headless browser). We must queue PDF jobs. Facts are checked by career-ops (it blocks
made-up numbers unless overridden). Effort: Low (wrapper to call CLI, mark PDF in UI).
Cover Letters (cover mode). Drafts a cover letter PDF based on job JD and profile. Integration: Similar to resume, call
career-ops cover --job <URL> --profile <file>, get a PDF. Expose JARVIS command /generate-cover <job>. Data: Job
and profile. Risks: Also heavy LLM/PDF work. Effort: Low.
Company Research (deep mode). Summarises company info (strategy, culture) and suggests how the user’s profile
fits. Integration: JARVIS adds /company-research <company> to call career-ops deep --company <name or URL>.

---

### [Page 59]
JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 59
This uses browser scrape and LLM. Data: Company name or URL. The result is text to display (could store in
knowledge memory). Risks: Web scraping can cause SSRF/XSS if not careful. We sandbox Playwright (it’s built-in),
and restrict to known research sites (LinkedIn, Crunchbase, news). Effort: Medium.
Contact Finder (contacto). Scrapes for hiring manager or recruiter contacts and drafts a brief LinkedIn message.
Integration: New command /find-contact <company> <role>. JARVIS feeds company/role and user networking
profile to career-ops contacto. Output is contact info + message. Data: Company, target role, optional user LinkedIn.
Risks: Privacy: scraping public profiles is allowed but respect site TOS. Sanitize any fetched names. Effort: Medium.
Email Drafts (email mode). Composes a formal application email with subject/body/attachment checklist.
Integration: /draft-email <application> command that supplies a report or JD to career-ops email. It returns a
ready-to-send email body. Effort: Low.
Application Analysis (analyze-patterns.mjs). Looks at past applications to find rejection trends or funnel stats.
Integration: JARVIS stores each application’s outcome in a local DB. We provide a command /analyze-applications
that reads JARVIS history and calls career-ops analyze-patterns (feeding it our pipeline file). It outputs stats or
charts. Risks: All data is user’s own history, kept local. Effort: Low.
Dashboard TUI (dashboard/). A terminal UI to browse/filter the application pipeline. Integration: JARVIS currently has
chat or GUI; we could optionally embed the career-ops Go-based TUI as a console feature. Alternatively, replicate
key pipeline views in JARVIS’s UI by reading the pipeline database. Using the existing TUI requires shipping the Go
binary. Effort: High if fully integrating; alternatively skip and use JARVIS’s own UI.
Interview Prep (interview/). Scripts for STAR answer templates, practice sessions, and flagging company red flags.
Integration: Add new commands or modes: /interview-prep <question> or /red-flags <company>. Call career-ops
interview/ scripts with the user’s story bank. Effort: Medium.
Offer Negotiation (offer-prep, salary-gap.mjs). Helps analyze offer letters and compare salary expectations.
Integration: Commands like /analyze-offer <offer-text>. Feed offer letter text to career-ops offer-prep and
salary-gap. Effort: Low.
Other (Plugins, Scanners, Follow-ups). career-ops has optional plugins (Gmail, Notion, etc.) and features like
follow-up cadences. We would initially disable plugins and use only core features to minimize complexity.

All career-ops modules are MIT-licensed (permissive) and run local-only. No new external services are introduced.
Integration Architecture and Code Changes
Invocation: We add a “Career-Ops” service layer in JARVIS. Whenever a relevant command is issued, JARVIS spawns
the career-ops CLI as a subprocess with appropriate flags. For example, in Python:
import subprocess, json
def run_job_scan(job_url, profile_md):
result = subprocess.run([
"career-ops", "scan",
"--job", job_url,

---

### [Page 60]
JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 60
"--profile", profile_md,
"--output-format", "json"
], capture_output=True, text=True)
if result.returncode != 0:
raise RuntimeError("Career-Ops scan failed")
data = json.loads(result.stdout)
return data
# Example usage:
scan_result = run_job_scan("https://jobs.example.com/123", "data/profile.yml")
print(f"Global fit score: {scan_result['global_score']}")
Similarly, JARVIS commands /generate-resume, /generate-cover, etc., will call career-ops generate-pdf, career-ops
cover, etc., capturing file outputs or text.
Data Adapters: JARVIS must translate its internal user profile (CV, experience) into career-ops’ expected format
(Markdown and profile.yml). We will maintain a shared workspace or export from JARVIS’s database into data/cv.md
and data/profile.yml. Conversely, career-ops outputs (JSON scores, generated PDFs, updated pipeline.md) must be
parsed back into JARVIS’s store. For instance, after scanning, JARVIS adds a new application entry with fields score,
job_title, etc., and attaches the PDF resume.
UI/Commands: We extend JARVIS’s interface (chat or CLI) with new commands:
/job-scan <job-url>
/generate-resume <job-url or ID>
/generate-cover <job-url or ID>
/find-contact <company> [role]
/company-research <company>
/draft-email <application-id>
/analyze-applications
/interview-prep [question]
/red-flags <company>
/analyze-offer <offer-id>
Each command triggers the above subprocess calls and returns results back to the user. Responses (scores,
summaries) are shown in JARVIS chat or UI panels. The new UI simply wraps the career-ops output.
Worker Pool: Scanning, LLM analysis, and PDF generation are CPU/memory-intensive (esp. launching Chromium). To
handle 10,000 users, we must limit concurrency. We will implement a job queue and a fixed pool of worker
processes or Docker containers. For example, only 3–5 parallel career-ops tasks at once. Excess requests queue up
or are rejected with a “please try later” message. In a web deployment, use a rate limiter per user and
circuit-breaker for resource exhaustion. This avoids crashes from mass requests.
Storage: We extend JARVIS’s local store with fields for application tracking (application status, scores, contact info,
resume/cover PDFs). If JARVIS currently uses a database (e.g. SQLite), recall that SQLite locks on writes (only one
writer at a time). With many users or parallel tasks, file contention can occur. Mitigation: a) Separate workspaces:
each user has their own career-ops folder, avoiding cross-talk. b) Migration to a DB: store pipeline data in a proper
DB table. If sticking with files, use locking or WAL mode (SQLite WAL can allow some concurrency).
Summary Architecture Diagram:
User Commands / GUI
|
v
JARVIS Core (Chatbot) + Task Dispatcher
| (spawns subprocess)
v
Career-Ops CLI (scan, pdf, etc.)
|
v
Local Workspace (profile.yml, cv.md, pipeline.md, PDFs)
|
v
AI Engine (Claude/Codex) & Playwright
|
v
Results (JSON scores, text, PDFs) back into JARVIS data store
No external APIs are called (except public job boards via career-ops scrapers). All processing is on-premise.
Security, Privacy, and Risks

---

### [Page 61]
JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 61
Local Data & Privacy. By design, all user data (CV, profile, job responses, resumes) remain on the user’s machine.
Career-ops explicitly has no telemetry or accounts. We must ensure JARVIS continues this policy: no user data leaves
the client. Even when scraping job sites or LinkedIn, we do so locally via Playwright (which can be sandboxed). No
API keys or credentials are stored beyond what the user provides locally (e.g. their profile).
SSRF and Unsafe URLs. When scraping or fetching URLs, attacker-controlled input could trigger Server-Side Request
Forgery (SSRF). To mitigate: validate URLs before passing to career-ops. Restrict scanning to known job board
domains (Glassdoor, LinkedIn, etc.) or allow only HTTP(S). The OWASP SSRF guide recommends whitelisting domains
or blocking private IPs. Additionally, Playwright (used by career-ops for verification and research) should run in
sandbox mode (no elevated privileges). Our implementation should honor career-ops’ built-in safeguards (it avoids
private IPs by default, as noted in its docs).
Input Sanitization. All inputs to career-ops (job descriptions, company names, previous answers) must be sanitized.
The risk is limited since outputs are local and not executed, but we should strip dangerous characters from job
descriptions or CV fields to avoid accidental CLI injection. Running career-ops via subprocess with fixed arguments
(as above) prevents shell injection.
Resource Exhaustion. A malicious user might attempt DoS by flooding the system with scan requests or many large
tasks. Mitigation: per-user rate limits; require CAPTCHAs or login for web UI; cap output sizes. The worker pool
approach also mitigates overload (excess jobs queue instead of crashing).
Dependency Security. Career-ops uses Node and Playwright; ensure we install them securely (prefer fixed versions,
audit NPM packages). Its open-source community is active, but we should review updates before adding as a library.
In summary, we add career-ops as a sandboxed, local plugin. We enforce URL validation and worker limits. Users
retain final control (JARVIS won’t auto-submit applications). Overall security remains strong, matching career-ops’s
own design.
Scalability and Load Testing (10k Users)
To survive 10k active users under potential hostile traffic:
Throttling & Queuing: As above, cap e.g. 5 simultaneous career-ops tasks globally. Use a queue (e.g. Redis-backed)
if requests spike. Any request beyond capacity is delayed or given an error message. This prevents CPU/Mem
blowout.
Load Testing: We will simulate high concurrency (e.g. with Locust or k6) to model 1k–10k users hitting scan/PDF
endpoints. Monitor CPU, memory, and task wait times. The goal is to find the breakpoints (e.g. how many concurrent
LLM calls can one machine handle). These tools allow testing patterns like “5,000 concurrent users performing a
scan”.
Benchmarks: For example, start with 100 users ramping up to 10k. Identify at what point latency degrades or errors
spike.
Results: If a single server cannot handle the load, we may shard the workload (multiple JARVIS instances) or require
more CPU. But the software architecture itself needs no fundamental changes beyond our caps.
Monitoring: In production, log resource usage per request. Use health checks: if career-ops tasks start failing due to
OOM or timeouts, trigger alerts.
Database: Under heavy load with many writes to the pipeline DB, SQLite might become a bottleneck. As above,
consider migrating to Postgres or another multi-writer DB for very large user bases. For now, we note it as a future
scale-up option.
Implementation Roadmap
Prototype Integration (Dev).
Add sample commands in JARVIS to call career-ops init/scan/pdf.
Test data flow: ensure JARVIS profile exports to profile.yml; capture scan JSON and PDF files.
Write unit tests mocking career-ops outputs (e.g. fake JSON) to ensure parsing logic works.
Security Review & Hardening.
Implement URL whitelisting and input validation (see OWASP SSRF guidelines).
Run static analysis on career-ops code for malicious patterns. Verify safe Playwright config.
Beta Testing (Canary).
Deploy to a small group of users or internal QA. Gather feedback on API commands.
Perform load tests with simulated users (100–1000 concurrent). Adjust queue limits.

JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 62
Documentation.
Update user manual: explain new commands (/career-ops scan, etc.) and required setup (npm install, Chromium).
Provide troubleshooting (e.g. “if scan fails, check network access to job sites”).
Performance Tuning.
Based on tests, tweak worker pool size. Optionally, containerize career-ops (each task in its own Docker).
Monitor disk I/O: career-ops does many file reads/writes. Ensure SSD or fast storage.
Rollout.
Feature-flag the integration so it can be disabled if issues arise.
Gradually enable for all users. Collect metrics on usage and errors.
Iterate on any failures (e.g. URL catch filters, or database locks).
A simplified plan avoids heavy refactoring: we keep most JARVIS logic intact and treat career-ops as a plugin. We
avoid rewriting career-ops functionality inside JARVIS. This minimizes breaking changes and leverages the existing
tested CLI.
Summary of Changes
New Commands: /job-scan, /generate-resume, /generate-cover, /company-research, /find-contact, /draft-email,
/analyze-applications, /interview-prep, /red-flags, /analyze-offer.
Backend: Spawn career-ops CLI as subprocess, use Python’s subprocess or equivalent.
Data: Export JARVIS profile to career-ops format; import JSON/PDF outputs back. Extend DB with application/tracker
fields.
Queue & Concurrency: Implement worker queue (e.g. Celery, RQ, or simple in-process queue) to limit parallel CLI
calls.
Security: Add URL validation (whitelist known domains) to avoid SSRF; sandbox browser jobs.
Testing: Write tests for each new command, simulate edge cases (bad URLs, missing CV). Conduct load tests (e.g.
with Locust).
Throughout, we clearly label any assumptions (e.g. JARVIS’s current data model) vs. known facts. The career-ops
repository confirms its features and license. We rely on that documentation to ensure compatibility and no license
conflicts.
Next Steps: Final Document Assembly
Once the above sections are written out (with citations and details), the content would be merged with the original
JARVIS blueprint document. In practice, one would use a tool like Pandoc or a PDF library to concatenate the existing
PDF and this new material into a final report. For example:
Generate the new content as PDF (via Markdown→PDF or LaTeX).
Use pdfjam, PyPDF2 or similar to append pages 1–51 (original) with pages 52–end (new content).
The user requested a merged PDF (“final_merge.pdf”) of ~200–600 pages. In reality, our detailed integration plan
above might be ~6–10 pages of text (far less than 200). The rest would be the original 40-page blueprint. (A
600-page PDF is not practical or necessary.) If truly desired, one might add extensive appendices (full docs, code
snippets, benchmark results, etc.), but the key is the substantive analysis given here.
Citations: All feature descriptions are drawn from the career-ops documentation. License and design (local run, no
telemetry) are confirmed by its site. Security notes reference OWASP’s SSRF guidance and SQLite concurrency
behavior. These ensure our plan is grounded in published facts.

How to read this catalog
The catalog keeps every power in the template you specified. Entries PWR-001 to PWR-015 carry detailed values
from the source catalog. Entries in the table sections carry the ID, name, priority and any approval/danger/example
values from the source; the remaining fields are filled from a category profile (for example, all IT-admin powers
inherit read-only telemetry, restricted shell, and 'fail closed' handling). Those profile-filled fields are sensible
defaults, not per-power research, so review them when you start building a power.
Each card shows a basis line saying which of the three cases applies. Approval and danger were raised automatically
for names that imply deletion, submission, login, purchase, deployment, killing processes or network changes.
Priority guide: P0 build first (foundation or safety), P1 core value, P2 later or situational, P3 experimental. Value
scores not given in the source are derived from priority (P0=9, P1=7, P2=6, P3=4).
Safety rules that apply to every power: deny by default, deterministic policy gate, approval bound to exact content,
audit log, kill switch, no secrets in prompts, web/email/document content is data not instructions.
Jarvis should be built as a modular personal AI operating system, not a single chatbot.
Voice / Text / Mobile / Dashboard
|
v
Jarvis Orchestrator
|
+-----------+-----------+------------+
|           |           |            |
Memory      Tools/MCP   Agent Pool   Safety Layer
|           |           |            |
Files      Browser     Researchers   Approvals
Screen     GitHub      Coders        Audit Log
Web        Servers     Analysts      Kill Switch
Home       Finance     Creators      Cost Governor
Core principles
1. Local-first: Private data, files, screen activity, and memory should remain local whenever possible.
2. Modular powers: Every capability is independently installable, disableable, testable, and auditable.
3. Fast path and smart path: Simple commands run instantly without an LLM; complex tasks use reasoning
models.
4. Human-in-the-loop: Dangerous actions require explicit approval.
5. Observable: Every tool call, decision, approval, and result is logged.
6. Cost-aware: Jarvis tracks model, API, bandwidth, and compute cost.
7. Fail safely: Every power has fallback, retry, timeout, and kill-switch behavior.
OpenJarvis similarly separates intelligence, inference engine, agents, tools/memory, and learning into composable
layers, which is the right architecture for Jarvis. open-jarvis.github

Page 91
5. Instant Local Command Powers
These should bypass the LLM when possible, like Atticus’s instant-command model.
PWR-027 Open app
P0
Spec basis: source catalog row; remaining fields filled from the category profile (review before build)
Power ID
PWR-027
Power name
Open app
Category
Instant Local Command
One-line description
Open app: instant deterministic OS command that bypasses the LLM.
User command examples
“Open VS Code”
Trigger types
Voice, text, schedule, event, or call from another power
Inputs
User request plus the data sources listed below
Outputs
Result with evidence/citation, spoken or written report, audit event
Required integrations
OS shell/AppleScript/PowerShell wrappers with allow-listed verbs
Required MCP
server/API/service
Filesystem MCP (scoped roots) or local command table
Data sources
Local file system, app registry
AI model role
Only for fuzzy name matching; the action itself is deterministic
Local/cloud preference
Local only
Permissions required
Scoped filesystem and app launch
Danger level
Low
Approval required?
No
Sandbox/simulation
mode
Dry-run prints the exact command without executing
Fallback behavior
Offer LLM-planned route if the verb is unknown
Failure handling
Report exact OS error; do not retry destructive verbs
Logging requirements
Verb, resolved target, exit code
Privacy considerations
Never reads file contents unless the verb needs it
Cost considerations
Zero
Implementation difficulty
Easy
Estimated build time
0.5-1 day
Dependencies
PWR-048 Run approved command (allow-list), PWR-288 Orchestrator (routing)
Related powers
PWR-028
Value score /10
9/10
Priority: P0 / P1 / P2 / P3
P0
Implementation notes
Reference implementations: Atticus (instant local commands without AI).
Example workflow
Utterance matches command table -> resolve target -> policy check -> execute -> speak result.

PWR-100 System health report

Spec basis: source catalog row; remaining fields filled from the category profile (review before build)
Power ID
PWR-100
Power name
System health report
Category
IT Administration
One-line description
System health report: read-first system administration and monitoring capability.
User command examples
Jarvis, system health report; Jarvis, schedule system health report daily at 9 AM (if it is a monitor)
Trigger types
Voice, text, schedule, event, or call from another power
Inputs
User request plus the data sources listed below
Outputs
Result with evidence/citation, spoken or written report, audit event
Required integrations
psutil/WMI/systemd, Docker socket (read-only), SSH to allow-listed hosts, notification channel
Required MCP
server/API/service
Docker MCP, Kubernetes MCP, Filesystem MCP (logs), SSH wrapper
Data sources
Host metrics, logs, service status, certificates, backups
AI model role
Correlates symptoms, proposes fixes, writes incident reports
Local/cloud preference
Local / on-prem
Permissions required
Read-only system telemetry; restricted shell for allow-listed commands
Danger level
Medium
Approval required?
Confirm before writing or changing anything
Sandbox/simulation
mode
Dry-run shows the command and expected effect; test on a staging VM
Fallback behavior
Return last good report marked stale
Failure handling
Alert on Telegram, include raw error, stop before any change
Logging requirements
Every probe/command with host, exit code, output hash
Privacy considerations
Logs may contain secrets and personal data: redact before any hosted model
Cost considerations
Low
Implementation difficulty
Medium
Estimated build time
1-3 days
Dependencies
PWR-100 System health report, PWR-298 Security guard, PWR-279 Approval gate, PWR-288 Orchestrator
(routing)
Related powers
PWR-101, PWR-285, PWR-279
Value score /10
9/10
Priority: P0 / P1 / P2 / P3
P0
Implementation notes
Reference implementations: OpenClaw (nodes, cron), Hermes Agent (terminal backends),
Docker/Kubernetes MCP servers, Home Assistant for sensors.
Example workflow
Schedule or voice trigger -> read-only probes -> compare to baseline -> report with evidence -> propose
fix -> approval -> apply -> verify.

Correlates symptoms, proposes fixes, writes incident reports
Local/cloud preference
Local / on-prem
Permissions required
Read-only system telemetry; restricted shell for allow-listed commands
Danger level
Medium
Approval required?
Confirm before writing or changing anything
Sandbox/simulation
mode
Dry-run shows the command and expected effect; test on a staging VM
Fallback behavior
Return last good report marked stale
Failure handling
Alert on Telegram, include raw error, stop before any change
Logging requirements
Every probe/command with host, exit code, output hash
Privacy considerations
Logs may contain secrets and personal data: redact before any hosted model
Cost considerations
Low
Implementation difficulty
Medium
Estimated build time
1-3 days
Dependencies
PWR-100 System health report, PWR-298 Security guard, PWR-279 Approval gate, PWR-288 Orchestrator
(routing)
Related powers
PWR-125, PWR-285, PWR-279
Value score /10
7/10
Priority: P0 / P1 / P2 / P3
P1
Implementation notes
Reference implementations: OpenClaw (nodes, cron), Hermes Agent (terminal backends),
Docker/Kubernetes MCP servers, Home Assistant for sensors.
Example workflow
Schedule or voice trigger -> read-only probes -> compare to baseline -> report with evidence -> propose
fix -> approval -> apply -> verify.

Result with evidence/citation, spoken or written report, audit event

server/API/service
Vault CLI wrapper; no credentials via prompts; Git/GitHub MCP for code scans

Vault metadata, code, dependency manifests, logs

Triage and explain findings; never sees raw secrets
Local/cloud preference
Local

Critical

Always (explicit)

mode
Scan fixtures and seeded fake secrets first

Report finding for manual action

Fail closed: deny when policy is unknown

Immutable audit events with policy version

Secrets never logged; redact on write

Low
Implementation difficulty
Hard

3-7 days

9/10

P0

command allow-list.

mode

Revert branch, keep logs

Medium

P0

server/API/service

Inbox scope the user authorises, contacts, drafts

Local drafting; transport uses provider API

Read scoped inbox; send only with hash-bound approval
Danger level
Medium
Approval required?
Yes
Sandbox/simulation
mode
Send to a test address only

Leave draft in AWAITING_APPROVAL
Failure handling
Do not auto-retry a send that may have succeeded

draft_hash, recipient, attachments, approval id, send receipt

Never forward private chats to other AI services without approval

Low

Medium

1-3 days

P0

gotoHuman-style approval inbox.

### [Page 279]
JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 279
15. Finance and Market Powers
Jarvis must provide research and decision support only. It must never autonomously trade, move money, or give
guaranteed financial advice.
PWR-215 Stock watchlist
P0
Spec basis: source catalog row; remaining fields filled from the category profile (review before build)
Power ID
PWR-215
Power name
Stock watchlist
Category
Finance and Market
One-line description
Stock watchlist: research and decision-support capability for Indian and global markets (no autonomous
trading).
User command examples
Jarvis, stock watchlist; Jarvis, schedule stock watchlist daily at 9 AM (if it is a monitor)
Trigger types
Voice, text, schedule, event, or call from another power
Inputs
User request plus the data sources listed below
Outputs
Result with evidence/citation, spoken or written report, audit event
Required integrations
Market-data source of the user's choice, watchlist DB, notification channel
Required MCP
server/API/service
Fetch MCP, spreadsheet/CSV tools; broker API read-only if the user opts in
Data sources
NSE/BSE and global quotes (verify provider terms), filings, annual reports, concall transcripts, user's
journal
AI model role
Summarises filings, explains metrics, tracks thesis vs outcome
Local/cloud preference
Local store; cloud data feeds
Permissions required
Network read; local DB write
Danger level
Medium
Approval required?
Confirm before writing or changing anything
Sandbox/simulation
mode
Paper portfolio only
Fallback behavior
Show last cached data labelled stale
Failure handling
Never act on stale or missing prices
Logging requirements
Data source, timestamp, calculation inputs
Privacy considerations
Portfolio holdings are private; keep local
Cost considerations
Low (data feed may cost)
Implementation difficulty
Medium
Estimated build time
1-3 days
Dependencies
PWR-215 Stock watchlist, PWR-279 Approval gate, PWR-288 Orchestrator (routing)
Related powers
PWR-216, PWR-285, PWR-279
Value score /10
9/10
Priority: P0 / P1 / P2 / P3
P0
Implementation notes
Reference implementations: Fetch MCP, LlamaIndex RAG for filings, n8n/cron for alerts.
Example workflow
Schedule -> pull data -> compare to alert rules -> summarise news/filings -> notify -> journal entry.
Jarvis never places orders.

19. Multi-Agent Powers
OpenJarvis includes orchestrator, deep-research, monitor-operative, persistent operative, and ReAct-style agents
across on-demand, scheduled, and continuous execution modes—an excellent model for Jarvis’s agent layer. github

Goal -> decompose to DAG -> launch independent nodes (max 2-3) -> collect artifacts -> verifier ->
report.

### [Page 392]
JARVIS Master Edition - Power Catalog, Career-Ops and GitHub Research
Page 392
Career-Ops powers (new)
Source: the two Career-Ops reports in Part B. Each power wraps one career-ops mode and keeps all data local.
PWR-328 Career-Ops service layer
P1
Spec basis: new power added from Career-Ops / researched GitHub projects / blueprint
Power ID
PWR-328
Power name
Career-Ops service layer
Category
Career-Ops
One-line description
Sandboxed worker pool that calls the career-ops CLI, maps JARVIS profile to cv.md/profile.yml and ingests
JSON/PDF results.
User command examples
Jarvis, set up career ops; Jarvis, export my profile for job scans
Trigger types
Voice, text, schedule, event, or call from another power
Inputs
User request plus the data sources listed below
Outputs
Result with evidence/citation, spoken or written report, audit event
Required integrations
career-ops CLI via subprocess in a sandboxed worker pool; workspace with cv.md and profile.yml
Required MCP
server/API/service
No MCP required; Playwright (bundled) for PDF; optional Gmail MCP for drafts
Data sources
User CV/profile Markdown, job URLs or pasted JDs, application pipeline
AI model role
LLM scores fit against CV and drafts documents; facts must come from the CV
Local/cloud preference
Local workspace; user's chosen model
Permissions required
Read CV; write workspace; network read on allow-listed job domains
Danger level
Medium
Approval required?
Confirm on first setup
Sandbox/simulation
mode
Run on sample CV and sample JDs; no submission
Fallback behavior
Return plain-text advice without CLI
Failure handling
Surface CLI exit code; never claim an application was submitted without a receipt
Logging requirements
Job URL, score, files created, approval ids
Privacy considerations
CV and salary data stay local; no telemetry
Cost considerations
Low-Medium (LLM tokens, Chromium CPU)
Implementation difficulty
Medium
Estimated build time
3-4 days
Dependencies
PWR-328 Career-Ops service layer, PWR-279 Approval gate, PWR-288 Orchestrator (routing)
Related powers
PWR-329, PWR-285, PWR-279
Value score /10
8/10
Priority: P0 / P1 / P2 / P3
P1
Implementation notes
Reference implementations: career-ops (as named in the supplied reports; verify repo before install),
Browser Use, Playwright. Source of this power: Career-Ops report.
Example workflow
Command -> validate URL against allow-list -> queue job -> career-ops CLI -> parse JSON/PDF -> save to
pipeline -> notify -> user approves any submission.

23. Safety Framework
Permission levels
Level
Access
0
Public web information
1
Read local non-sensitive files
2
Write to approved folders/apps
3
Send messages/emails
4
Modify system settings
5
Delete, deploy, purchase, or irreversible actions
Approval rules
• File deletion: Always approve
• Email/message sending: Always approve
• Form submission: Always approve
• Purchase/checkout: Always approve + explicit confirmation
• Server configuration change: Always approve
• Network configuration change: Always approve
• Code deployment: Always approve
• Read-only research: No approval
Mandatory controls
• Tool allow-lists
• Folder allow-lists
• Domain allow-lists
• Command allow-lists
• Rate limits
• Cost limits
• Sandbox execution
• Audit logging
• Kill switch
• Session expiry
• Secret redaction

26. Final Build Order
1. Build the brain: orchestrator, memory, tool gateway.
2. Add the senses: voice, screen, files, web.
3. Add the hands: browser, filesystem, Git, servers, automation.
4. Add judgment: approvals, audit trail, permissions, kill switch.
5. Add specialists: researcher, coder, sysadmin, finance, content agents.
6. Add autonomy: schedules, proactive alerts, background agents.

D4. Safe adoption checklist
• Several projects install with a curl-piped shell script (Atticus, OpenClaw, Hermes Agent). Download and read the
script first, then run it in a VM or separate OS user.
• Pin a release or commit; do not track main for anything with shell, file or browser access.
• Check telemetry: Atticus discloses a daily anonymous usage ping; Career-Ops reports describe no telemetry.
Confirm in code.
• Community MCP servers are untested and unaffiliated with Anthropic. Review tools and scopes (PWR-345) and
give each server only the folders, domains and credentials it needs.
• Do not expose agent gateways or control ports (OpenClaw gateway default port 18789, browser debug ports) to
the public internet; use a private mesh VPN.
• Pick ONE orchestrator. Mixing OpenClaw, Hermes, LangGraph and CrewAI at once makes logs, permissions and
debugging incoherent (blueprint p.6).
• Prefer reuse over rewrite: OpenClaw or Hermes Agent for gateway/skills, Playwright for web, local
faster-whisper/Piper for voice, MCP servers for tools, your own policy gate on top.

Scope note on the 10,000-hostile-user audit
Part A's stress audit assumes a multi-tenant cloud service. Your blueprint describes a private single-user system. For
JARVIS as specified, the parts that still matter are: authentication on every remote command, request rate limits,
queue caps (2-3 reasoning workers, one foreground desktop controller), cost limits and backpressure. Load
balancers, database sharding and autoscaling become relevant only if you later offer JARVIS to other people.
Permission mapping: blueprint Tier 0 (observe) = catalog levels 0-1; Tier 1 (reversible) = level 2; Tier 2 (explicit
approval) = level 3; Tier 3 (step-up) = levels 4-5.

Definition of done for any power
• Trigger, inputs, preconditions, permissions, environment, verification test, output artifact, failure path and
rollback are all written down (blueprint p.3).
• A dry-run mode exists, an audit event is written, and the kill switch stops it.
• Irreversible actions are at-most-once and bound to an approval hash.
• No success is claimed without evidence: a receipt, screenshot, saved file, diff or test output.

Original research brief
You are an elite AI research team, product strategist, software architect, automation engineer, cybersecurity
engineer, UX designer, and technical specification writer.
Your mission is to perform exhaustive deep research and create the definitive, implementation-ready catalog of
every meaningful “power” my personal Iron Man-style AI assistant, JARVIS, should have.

Read it fully before researching.
Extract its current vision, features, architecture, technologies, constraints, and missing capabilities.

Do not repeat obvious ideas.
Do not create vague powers such as “AI productivity booster.”
Every power must be specific, implementable, and useful in real daily life.
